15 Quant perspective: Reducing Equity 
Overweight as Valuation Weakened

Francis Lim Senior Quantitative Strategist Maggie, Au Yeung Quantitative Analyst

### Page 22

Global Market Outlook 22 
Fig. 36 Long- and short-term quantitative models remain bullish on risk assets 
Long-term models below have a typical time horizon of 3-6 months, while short-term models have a 1-3-month horizon

Long-term Stock or bond US recession model 
Current view Reduce equity Overweight 20% probability within the next 6 months 
What factors 
is this view 
based on? 
• Fundamental: +2. 3-month change in PMI new 
orders indicate a slower pace in the expansion of 
industrial activity. All other factors such as net 
earnings upgrades, economic surprise index and 
risk sentiment remain positive.  
• Valuation: -2. Both DM and Asian equities are 
expensive as their valuation scores exceeded 1 
standard deviation of historical average.  
• Technical: +1. Net-advances in global stocks are 
positive but not extreme. Market breadth declined 
as equity markets above their 200-day moving 
averages fell from 85% to 72%, but it remains 
above the model’s 60% threshold. 
Estimated probability hovers around 20 – 21% 
• Conference Board consumer confidence 
remains weak at -8.6% y/y. 
• PMI new orders-to-inventory ratio is at 1.1x, 
below its historical average of 1.2x.  
• Conference Board leading indicators 
contracted by -1.1% y/y.  
• Fed funds rate-to-natural rate ratio is still high 
at 1.6x, reflecting a restrictive monetary policy 
compared to historical average of 1.3x. 
Key model 
factors 
• Economic activity, macro risk and surprise indices, 
corporate earnings, forward P/E ratio and technical 
factors. 
• Conference Board leading indicator, Consumer 
Confidence Index, ISM New Orders and Fed 
funds rate. 
How does it 
work? 
• A monthly scorecard of -5 to 5 based on 
fundamentals, valuations and market breadth 
factors to indicate the relative preference for bonds 
and equities. A positive score favours equities and 
vice versa. 
• A probability model based on regression. Long-
term statistical tests are conducted to identify 
the four key economic factors above.

Short-term Technical analysis Investor positioning 
Current views Bullish S&P 500 & MSCI AC World indices None flagged 
What factors 
is this view 
based on? 
• Bullish S&P500 and MSCI AC World. The 
probabilities of bear market sit at just 0.7% and 
5.0%, respectively, driven by subdued options-
market implied volatility. Momentum across both 
indices have weakened but they point to a typical 
market consolidation rather than signs of meaningful  
market stress.  
• Previous long positioning in the USD index 
(DXY) has fully unwound after DXY fell 
steeply form its peak in late July. The renewed 
strength in DXY since the Fed’s 16 Sept rate 
hike has started from neutral positioning.  
• Market diversity indicator for MSCI 
Singapore has normalised after the index 
corrected from its September peak. 
Key model 
factors 
• Market factors: Momentum, volatility, interest rate 
differentials, relative returns, inflation swap rates, 
economic surprises, etc. 
• Price action: Overbought conditions occur 
when prices rise sharply; oversold conditions 
happen when prices fall rapidly in a short time.  
How does it 
work? 
• Scanning through 7,000+ factors, the framework 
uses Machine Learning to forecast market regimes 
or future trends based on identified market drivers. 
• A market indicator based on fractal analysis 
that provides a timely indication of investor 
positioning based on price actions.

Source: Standard Chartered

Time is your most precious commodity – be sure to spend it wisely 
Time is undoubtedly valuable. The days may seem long, but the years are short. So, make the choice 
to spend your time wisely. Whether you’re setting out on your investment journey, navigating the 
intricacies of mid -life wealth planning or fortifying assets  for your golden years, invest time today to 
ensure your wealth strategy is aligned to what’s right for you – Today, Tomorrow and Forever. 
Setting aside the time now to review your plan will pay dividends in the future. Markets have moved. 
Your portfolio’s current asset allocation may no longer be optimally positioned to maximise the 
opportunities ahead. Ask yourself the following: Am I holding too much cash? Am I sufficiently allocating 
to growth assets for the long term? Is my portfolio diversified? Am I capturing the best opportunities? 
And most importantly, is my wealth working hard for me so that I don’t have to? 
Use our SC Wealth Select framework and advisory specialists to help guide you through this process.

Purpose 
Today,  
Tomorrow,  
Forever 
Our approach to helping you grow and manage 
your wealth starts with you. We use a goals -
aware approach to understand your vision of 
Today, Tomorrow and Forever for yourself, your 
family and beyond, and then design portfolios to 
meet your various needs.  
Using our ‘Today, Tomorrow and Forever’ 
approach, we ensure your wealth needs for the 
near term (Today) are met, while ensuring your 
wealth needs for the decades ahead (Tomorrow 
and Forever) are also planned for.  
Your vision of ‘Today, Tomorrow and Forever’ is 
unique to you. Our specialists partner with you to 
build well -diversified, long -term Foundation 
portfolios, aligned to your Today, Tomorrow and 
Forever needs. Opportunistic ideas are added to 
capture short -term opportunities, a nd sufficient 
protection is included to address the objectives of 
you and your family. 
Today, Tomorrow and Forever 
Approach 
Planning for Today 
Requires ensuring liquidity and income 
flows take centre stage 
Securing Tomorrow 
Entails a well-diversified investment and 
protection portfolio with a focus on growth, 
ensuring inflation is accounted for and risks 
are mitigated 
Building for Forever 
Involves greater focus on long-term returns 
given the time horizon of your portfolio can 
be measured in decades, and might also 
include business interests, real estate, 
collectibles or charitable funds

SC Wealth Select  
 Managing your wealth through the decades – Today, Tomorrow and Forever

### Page 28

Global Market Outlook 28

Principles 
that stand the 
test of time 
Adhering to time-tested principles, to ensure your 
investment decisions remain robust and 
consistently applied, is paramount to your 
success Today, Tomorrow and Forever. We use 
five Wealth Principles to guide and guardrail your 
wealth decisions.

Discipline – ensure consistency and 
prudence over your emotions 
• Reacting to emotions, such as optimism and 
fear, can lead to poor investment decisions at 
the worst times 
• Have a plan and stick to it – this helps you to 
stay focused on the bigger picture

Diversification – simply put, don’t put 
all your eggs in one basket 
• Reduce risk by holding a variety of financial 
assets. Multi -asset diversification in your 
Foundation portfolio is important 
• As a guide, make sure your portfolio contains 
a variety of asset classes and investments 
that have low correlation with one another

Time in the Market – a more robust 
strategy than timing the market

• Predicting market selloffs is challenging, and 
timing your exit and re-entry is difficult 
• Missing out on the best performing days of a 
market can have a significantly detrimental 
impact on your portfolio 
• ‘Time in the market’ and buying the market 
with a longer -term view provide more 
consistent returns that can ride out bumps 
along the way

Risk and Return – make sure the risk 
is worth the return 
• To achieve higher investment returns, you will 
likely have to accept a greater level of risk in 
your portfolio 
• Therefore, it’s important to understand the 
risks and manage these on an ongoing basis

Protection – don’t let the unexpected 
catch you unprepared 
• Even though you may feel healthy, or 
financially stable now, protection offers the 
ability to overcome times of financial 
uncertainty and mitigate the long-term impact 
of unforeseen events on your wealth 
• A good protection plan not only safeguards 
your wealth today, but also considers the 
value of your future earnings over your 
lifetime, in today’s terms

Advisory 
Process 
Following a 
holistic 
approach to 
managing  
your wealth 
We follow a rigorous process to ensure your 
needs and objectives are well -understood, and 
your portfolio is well-aligned and manage s to  
deliver on these objectives. 
However, markets constantly evolve and your 
needs change. Hence, we encourage you to 
undertake regular portfolio reviews to ensure your 
portfolio remains aligned to your Today, 
Tomorrow and Forever objectives. This proactive 
approach includes strategic reb alancing based 
on insights from our Chief Investment Office.

Document 389: Quantum dictionary, part 2_ From potential to progress We go beyond the quantum basics to explore what’s next, from emerging applications to the global race for leadership. 2 min r

Source PDF: all report pdf/Quantum dictionary, part 2_ From potential to progress We go beyond the quantum basics to explore what’s next, from emerging applications to the global race for leadership. 2 min read.pdf

### Page 1

13037420     1

Transformation 
Quantum dictionary, part 2: From potential to progress 
02 November 2026

Key takeaways 
• In the second and final part of our quantum dictionary series, we move beyond the basics to explore where quantum could go 
next. Today's quantum computers remain limited by noise and errors, but their potential grows rapidly as they scale. Emerging 
applications like quantum AI and sensing could eventually unlock new capabilities and significant economic value. 
• Quantum communication could change both how we connect quantum computers and how we protect information. A quantum 
internet could use entanglement to link quantum systems, creating additional computing power, while quantum networks could 
make eavesdropping detectable and offer new ways to securely transmit information. 
• Meanwhile, the race for quantum leadership is already underway. Governments are pursuing greater quantum sovereignty as the 
technology's potential implications for competitiveness, security and defense grow.

Assessing quantum’s performance and progress 
Quantum advantage versus supremacy 
Quantum advantage is reached when a quantum computer can solve a useful, real-world problem faster than a classical 
computer. The problem is, today’s quantum computers are still too noisy, as we discussed in part one of this series, and qubit 
quality isn’t yet high enough to demonstrate quantum advantage.  
Quantum supremacy takes this one step further. It refers to the point when a quantum computer has computational advantage, 
meaning it can solve a problem that a traditional computer couldn’t in any feasible amount of time.  
Quantum scaling 
Quantum scaling is about building bigger, more powerful quantum computers. It involves overcoming the challenges of 
increasing both the number and the quality of qubits. Today, we’re in the noisy intermediate-scale quantum era. Quantum 
computers have tens to a few hundred qubits, but noise and errors still limit what they can do.1 
However, when quantum computers do scale, they do so exponentially. Every additional qubit doubles the number of operations 
the system can perform. A quantum computer with just 10 qubits could perform more than 100 times the operations of a 
classical computer (Exhibit 1). More qubits could therefore mean more operations and, ultimately, more economic value in the 
industries where quantum computing can be put to work, according to BofA Global Research. 
Exhibit 1: A quantum computer with just 10 qubits could perform more than 100 times the operations than classical 
Number of classical versus quantum operations for a given (qu)bit number (log scale)

Quantum Benchmarking Initiative 
The Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency’s (DARPA’s) Quantum Benchmarking Initiative (QBI) aims to answer a big 
question: Is it possible to build a commercially relevant quantum computer by 2033? To be clear, QBI is not a competition 
between companies. DARPA evaluates all viable approaches for which funding is available.  
QBI has three stages:  
• Stage A: Start with a concept. At this stage, there is a utility-scale quantum computer concept that has a plausible path to 
realization in the near term. 
• Stage B: Put a plan behind it. This stage involves developing a research and development plan, identifying the associated 
risks and introducing prototypes to overcome them. 
• Stage C: Put it to the test. In the final stage, companies work with the government to verify and validate that the utility-
scale quantum computer concept can be built as intended. 
Exploring emerging quantum applications  
Quantum AI 
According to BofA Global Research, quantum AI brings together two powerful technologies: AI and quantum computing, with the 
potential to improve both the physical and the digital worlds. And the relationship could work both ways. Quantum computing 
could provide some of the compute power needed to train generative AI, while generative AI could help speed up the 
development and testing time of quantum systems.  
AI tools like machine learning (ML) could also make quantum error correction (QEC) more efficient and scalable.2 ML models 
trained on simulated datasets can identify and correct errors faster than traditional methods while using fewer computing 
resources. They can also more accurately predict errors before they happen.3 
Quantum sensing 
Quantum sensing uses the properties of quantum mechanics to measure things like magnetic fields, gravitational forces, time 
and rotation with greater precision than classical instruments (Exhibit 2).4 
Exhibit 2: Quantum sensors use quantum properties to measure physical quantities with unmatched precision 
An infographic of quantum sensing

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

2 Wang, Z. & Tang, H. (2024, December 29). Artificial Intelligence for Quantum Error Correction: A Comprehensive Review. arXiv. 
3 Swayne, M. (2025, January 6). AI for Quantum Error Correction: A Comprehensive Guide to Using Artificial Intelligence to Improve Quantum Error Correction. 
Quantum Insider.  
4 Rehman, M.U. (2026, March 2). Understanding Quantum Sensing and Its Industrial Potential. Quantum Insider.

### Page 3

02 November 2026    3

I NSTI TUTE

So, how does it work? In simple terms, there are three steps. First, a quantum system is prepared in a well-defined initial state, 
for example, by cooling an atom to near absolute zero. Next, the sensor is exposed to whatever is being measured, causing a 
subtle shift in its quantum state. In an atomic magnetometer, for example, a magnetic field changes the energy levels of an 
atom’s electrons, which changes how the atom absorbs or emits light. Finally, the sensor measures how much the quantum state 
has shifted, revealing the strength of the external force.5  
Benefits and challenges 
Compared with quantum computers, quantum sensors face some lower technical barriers. They don’t need nearly as many qubits 
to be useful, entanglement can help but isn’t required for every application, and even short coherence times can produce useful 
measurements.6 The catch? They often require large vacuum chambers, laser systems and magnetic shielding to maintain 
coherence. 
The quantum internet and communications  
Quantum communication 
Quantum communication is the study of how information can be encoded and transmitted between quantum systems over a 
distance (Exhibit 3). It uses properties like superposition and entanglement to enable the secure transmission of information. 
Why does that matter? One of the main goals is to keep information secure from potential eavesdroppers, particularly as more 
powerful quantum computers could pose new challenges for traditional communication methods. The ultimate goal is what some 
call the quantum internet, where quantum signals can move seamlessly across many quantum networks.7 
Exhibit 3: Quantum communication uses quantum states to encode and transmit information 
An infographic of quantum communication

Source: BofA Global Research  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Quantum internet 
Think of the quantum internet as a scalable network that allows two or more quantum devices to exchange information (Exhibit 
4). These connections could span vastly different distances, from within a silicon chip all the way to satellite links.8 
But the quantum internet isn’t meant to replace the internet we use today, nor is it simply a faster version of it. According to 
Cisco, the classical internet can move information between computers, but it is ultimately limited by what those computers can 
compute. Some problems become so complex so quickly that no classical machine can solve them in a useful amount of time.

5 Ibid.  
6 Ibid.  
7 Quantinuum. (2024, July 30). Coming Over the Horizon: Quantum Communication Enters the Mainstream. 
8 Imperial. (n.d.). Quantum Internet.

### Page 4

4 02 November 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

When connection becomes computation 
Here’s what makes the quantum internet different: the connection itself can create new computing power. Using entanglement, 
a quantum network can connect quantum systems into a single, larger computing system that scales exponentially.9 That’s 
fundamentally different from classical computers, which communicate by passing copies of information back and forth. Quantum 
computers connected through a quantum network can instead create a feedback loop of entanglement and a shared space of 
possibilities that neither could produce on its own. 
Different hardware from classical 
Building a quantum internet requires a different set of hardware than the routers and switches that power today’s internet. Key 
pieces include:10   
• Quantum communication channels: These carry quantum signals, typically through fiber-optic cables or satellite links 
using free-space optics. 
• Quantum memory: To keep the network in sync, the system must catch a photon and store its quantum state in a 
stationary atom without destroying it. 
• Quantum repeaters: These help quantum signals travel longer distances by amplifying signals and reducing error rates. 
According to QuEra, the lack of viable quantum repeaters is currently a key limitation to building a global quantum 
network. 
Exhibit 4: A network of quantum computers which could send and receive information encoded in quantum states 
An infographic explaining the quantum internet

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Quantum networking 
Quantum networking is a way of sending information using quantum states. It generally includes three network types: quantum 
key distribution (QKD) for secure communications (more on this below), the quantum internet for distributed quantum 
computing, and quantum sensing networks that use entanglement to improve measurements.  
Classical networks turn information into electrical or optical signals that can be copied or intercepted without the sender or 
receiver knowing. Quantum networks work differently. Because information is transmitted using quantum states, it cannot be

9 Chan, M. (2026, June 1). Quantum internet 101: What it is, and why it matters. Cisco. 
10 QuEra. (n.d.) Quantum Internet.

### Page 5

02 November 2026    5

I NSTI TUTE

intercepted and copied without disturbing the original information. That disturbance makes an eavesdropping attempt 
detectable. 
Evaluating security & strategic risks 
Quantum encryption 
Quantum encryption uses the principles of quantum mechanics, including superposition and entanglement, to encrypt and 
securely transmit data (Exhibit 5). 
One potential risk of quantum computing is that it could eventually break widely used forms of encryption. And while large-scale 
quantum computers are still being developed, organizations may need to start preparing for that possibility today.  
Exhibit 5: Examples include quantum key distribution and post-quantum cryptography 
An infographic illustrating quantum encryption

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Quantum key distribution 
Quantum key distribution (QKD), a type of quantum encryption, uses the laws of quantum physics to create and distribute secure 
keys which prevent data decryption.11 Think of a key as something that locks and unlocks a digital message. Two parties share a 
key to encrypt and decrypt information sent between them. If an eavesdropper tries to obtain the key, the attempt disturbs the 
quantum states, alerting the parties that the key may have been compromised and shouldn’t be used.12 
According to Quantum Insider, QKD generally involves three steps:13 
• Create entangled photons: First, a quantum source, such as a laser and nonlinear crystal or a quantum dot, generates pairs 
of entangled photons. The photons are correlated in ways that classical light cannot replicate, so measurements of one 
reveal information about its partner.  
• Send the photons: The photons travel to different locations through fiber-optic cables or free-space optical channels, 
such as satellite-to-ground or satellite-to-satellite links, while maintaining their quantum correlations. But those 
connections can be lost over long distances, with current systems reaching roughly 100 kilometers, according to Quantum 
Insider.14 Classical networks get around distance limitations with repeaters that receive, amplify and retransmit signals.

11 Toshiba. (n.d.). Quantum Key Distribution (QKD). 
12 QuEra. (n.d.). Quantum Key Distribution. 
13 Rehman, M.U. (2026, March 9). Understanding Quantum Networking and Its Industrial Potential. Quantum Insider. 
14 Ibid.

### Page 6

6 02 November 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

Quantum signals can’t simply be amplified without destroying their quantum properties, so quantum repeaters take a 
different approach. They use entanglement swapping and quantum memory to extend the range of quantum networks. 
• Measure the photons: Finally, the photons are measured at each location. This confirms whether the entanglement 
survived the journey and can reveal whether someone tried to intercept the photons along the way. The results can then 
be used to generate a shared secret key for encrypting classical messages. 
Quantum sovereignty 
Quantum sovereignty is an economic actor’s ability to develop, produce and operate its own quantum technology.  
Quantum is the first new computing platform technology since the arrival of digital computing. And the stakes are high. 
Stakeholders are interested in quantum because of its potential implications for economic competitiveness, security, supply 
chain resilience and defense. As a result, some may pursue “sovereign quantum,” meaning quantum capabilities are owned and 
operated by a particular nation. That could involve investing in quantum technology, building domestic expertise or even export 
controls to limit other stakeholders’ ability to develop their own sovereign capabilities.  
The geopolitical race is on 
Government quantum investment has reached at least about $58 billion cumulatively as of 2025, led by Europe at $15.4 billion 
and China at $15.3 billion.  
The competition extends beyond investment. At least nine countries implemented export controls on quantum technology in 
2024. Over the past 20 years, China and the US have led in patent requests filed, but Europe and China have led in the number 
of patents granted. The race for talent is global, too. China has trained the largest share of top quantum researchers at the 
undergraduate level, at roughly one-fourth, while the US is home to the largest share of top talent by employment, at one-third.  
Quantum supply chain 
Building quantum technology requires much more than qubits. A broader supply chain provides the equipment, components and 
materials needed to make quantum systems work, including: 
• Cryogenics: Most types of qubits require some level of extreme cooling, with superconducting qubits among the most 
demanding at roughly 5–10 millikelvin. Keeping qubits this cold helps shield them from environmental noise that can 
cause decoherence. 
• Qubit control electronics: Quantum gates direct qubits by sending them photons. That requires supporting electronics, 
including wiring, room-temperature electronics and amplifiers. For example, analog microwave signals used to read qubits 
must be amplified through several stages before they can be converted into digital signals and analyzed. 
• Photonics: Lasers use light and atoms to help power quantum technology, while other devices can modify properties of 
photons, such as their path and polarization. 
• Foundries: Quantum development also relies on several types of fabrication facilities, including research fabs at national 
research organizations and universities, pre-industry research fabs, independent foundries that manufacture 
semiconductors for third parties, and in-house fabs operated by quantum technology vendors. 
• Raw materials: Quantum systems also depend on materials used in qubits themselves and in supporting components such 
as cables and cryostats. Helium-3 and helium-4 are used in cryostats to maintain very low temperatures, while rare earth 
metals used in quantum technology include yttrium, neodymium, europium, erbium and ytterbium.15 
AI singularity 
AI singularity refers to a hypothetical point when AI evolves to become self-improving and autonomous, surpassing human 
cognitive abilities.16  
A complementary relationship between quantum and AI 
Rather than developing in isolation, AI and quantum computing could ultimately help advance each other. Quantum computing 
may eventually provide some of the compute power needed to train generative AI, while generative AI could help speed up the 
development and testing of quantum systems.

15 Swayne, M. (2025, October 15). Rare-Earth Minerals And Quantum Computing: How Secure Are The Supply Chains? Quantum Insider. 
16 ISB. (2025, March). What is AI Singularity? Explore Its Future Impact.

### Page 7

02 November 2026    7

I NSTI TUTE

That relationship could become increasingly important as the computing power needed to train the largest generative AI models 
continues to grow exponentially. Classical computing may eventually struggle to keep pace, while quantum computing could help 
meet some of that demand because its computing power also scales exponentially. 
By 2045? 2029? Now? 
AI is becoming increasingly powerful and capable, raising a big question: when could it reach or even surpass human intelligence? 
Futurologist Ray Kurzweil expects AI to reach human levels of intelligence by 2029 and technological singularity by 2045. His 
view extends Moore’s Law beyond processing power to other forms of technological progress. 
Under that framework, computing power would continue growing exponentially. By 2045, Kurzweil projects that $1,000 worth of 
computing could perform as many calculations in one second as all human brains combined.

～追加利上げの時期や総選挙を巡る不透明感が重なり、豪ドルとの対照的な動きが続くか～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ ニュージーランド準備銀行（RBNZ）は、10 月 27～28 日に開催した定例理事会において、政
策金利を2.75％に据え置くことを決定した。RBNZ は７月に約３年ぶりの利上げに舵を切り、
９月にも追加利上げを実施するなど金融引き締めを強めてきた。このため、今回の据え置き
は３会合ぶりであり、金融市場の事前予想に反する決定となった。 
○ ７-９月のインフレ率は前年比＋3.7％と前期から鈍化したものの、コアインフレ率は加速す
るなど、インフレ圧力は依然として根強いことが確認された。このため、金融市場において
は RBNZ が今回利上げに動くことを織り込む動きが広がりをみせていた。こうしたなか、 今回
の決定は４対３の僅差となったほか、声明文や総裁会見でも追加利上げの必要性が示された
ものの、そのタイミングについては不透明感が残る。 
○ また、11 月７日の総選挙を控え、RBNZ の政策目標の見直しが争点となっていることも、 先行
きの政策運営を巡る不透明感につながっている。金融市場では、予想外の金利据え置きに対
する 「失望」 に加え、 金融政策が後手に回るとの警戒感もＮＺドル相場の重しとなっている。
当面はＮＺドル相場の上値が重い展開が続くと見込まれ、オセアニア通貨のなかでも豪ドル
とＮＺドルが対照的な動きをみせる可能性がある。

ーインフレを招いたことから、RBNZ は７月の定例会合で約３年ぶりの利上げに舵を切り、 ９月会合
でも追加利上げに動くなど、金融引き締めを強化してきた（注１）。このため、RBNZ による金利据

上昇が抑制されており、 中期的なインフレ期待は安定している」 とする一方、 「コアインフレ率は目
標レンジの上限近くに加速しており、物価上昇の動きが広範に 及びつつある」との見方を示した。
先行きについては「世界的な情勢が短期的にインフレ圧力を一段と強めている」として、その要因
に「原油高によるエネルギー価格の上昇と通貨ＮＺドル安」 を挙げた。 そして、 「10-12 月のインフ
レ率は＋4.4％に加速が見込まれる」としつつ、 「原油や石油製品価格の変動が激しく、インフレ見
通しは不透明」との見方を示した。 
実体経済については「回復が続いているものの、状況には依然ばらつきがある」との見方を示し
た。具体的には「外需関連産業では、商品市況の上昇、世界的な需要の堅調さ、外国人観光客数の
増加の恩恵を受けている」一方、 「内需関連産業は低調に推移している」とした。一方で、 「オーク
ランドを中心に住宅投資の回復の兆しがでている」とした。 
政策運営については「物価に対する一時的な要因を差し引いた上で、 経済に不必要な不安定を起
こすことを回避しつつ、中期的にインフレ率を目標レンジの中央（２％）に戻すことに注力してい
る」とあらためて強調した。 そのうえで、 「家計消費が弱く、 一部の企業ではコスト上昇の転嫁が制
限されている」 としつつ、 「インフレ率が高止まりすれば、 コスト上昇がインフレ期待や価格や賃金
に波及してインフレがより持続的なものとなるリスクが高まる」との見方を示した。 
世界経済については「堅調に推移しているものの、地政学リスクやエネルギーインフレ、金融市
場のボラティリティの高まり、ＡＩ（人工知能）関連投資の急増などを背景に、その脆弱性は高ま
っている」との見方を示した。一方で、同国経済について「回復の動きが予想以上に力強くなる可
能性はある一方、世界経済の悪化、外需の好調さが雇用や所得、家計消費に波及しなければ勢いを
失う可能性がある」との認識を示した。そのうえで、今回の決定について「政策委員は全体的なイ
ンフレ見通しでは一致したが、個々のリスクに対する見方や金融政策の対応時期に関する意見は割
れた」、先行きについても「追加利上げが必要となる可能性は高いとの見方で一致したものの、 その
タイミングは経済見通し次第」として、追加利上げに含みを持たせる考えを示した。 
会合後に記者会見に臨んだブレマン総裁は、今回の協議について「景気動向を勘案すれば、より
抑制的な政策が必要になることで合意した」 、 「ＮＺドル安が続けば輸入インフレを助長する可能性
がある」との認識が共有されたとした。先行きについては「さらなる引き締めが必要となり、状況
によっては引き締め的な領域に入る可能性がある」 、 「今後の会合で追加利上げを行う可能性が高い」
との認識を示した。そして、 「ＯＣＲの道筋が９月会合の想定を上回る可能性についても協議した」
としつつ、 「中立金利の見通しには幅があり、 常に不確実性がある」 として、 具体的な水準への言及
は避けた。一方で、 今回の決定については「経済見通しに基づいた決定である」 としたうえで、 「イ

Portfolio Strategist

Islamic finance is growing at a rapid pace, driven 
primarily by increasing demand for investors who 
seek Shariah -compliant investments. Sukuk, often 
referred to as Islamic bonds, surpassed USD 1trn in 
2025, making them one of the fastest -growing 
segments o f Islamic finance. The asset class’s 
unique value proposition is driving its popularity, 
especially in an increasingly volatile global  
economic landscape. 
In this publication, we will explore the evolving role of Sukuk in wealth management, 
examining market fundamentals, risk frameworks and emerging opportunities within 
this market.

### Page 3

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026  3

Sukuk, commonly referred to as Islamic bonds, are Shariah-compliant investment 
certificates representing ownership interests in underlying assets, usufructs (the 
right to use an asset and receive its benefits, without owning it) or ventures. In 
return, Sukuk holders receive periodic distributions, typically in the form of rental 
payments (Ijarah), profit-sharing (Mudarabah) or asset-generated cash flows, and 
upon maturity the principal amount is repaid. Sukuk differ from conventional 
bonds in several ways: 
Fig. 1  
Sukuk vs. conventional bonds

Aspect  Conventional bonds Sukuk

Core principle  Lending (time-value of money) Asset ownership 
Underlying asset  Any asset, business or project Must be Sharia-compliant 
Returns  Guaranteed (fixed or floating interest) Not legally guaranteed (depends on 
underlying asset’s performance) 
Risk   Issuer bears all performance risk Shared risk between parties 
Transparency  Moderate (depends on disclosure) High (asset identification required)

Source: Standard Chartered

The role of Sukuk in the investing landscape is evolving amid 
global uncertainty and rising geopolitical tensions. Investors 
are increasingly seeking more stable and asset ‑backed 
alternatives. An investor can consider investing in Sukuk for 
the following reasons: 
Resilient 
Sukuk provide asset -backed collateral that conventional 
bonds often lack, offering a cushion during volatile market 
conditions. Historically, Islamic investments, including Sukuk’, 
have been countercyclical, i.e. they tend to expand or remain 
stable when c onventional markets contract. Across major 
market stress from the 2018 US-China Trade War through the 
2026 Middle East Conflict, Sukuk bonds consistently 
outperformed Global Bonds — delivering smaller drawdowns 
in risk-off episodes such as the 2018 US-China trade war and 
2022 higher for longer inflation, and greater resilience in 
geopolitical oil shocks, underscoring the asset class's 
structural defensive characteristics.

1 The evolving role of Sukuk

Strong fundamentals
The Sukuk market experienced an unprecedented 25% growth in issuance in 2025, with issuance nearly USD 300bn. According
to some  reports,  the  Sukuk  market  could  reach  USD  3trn  by  2030  driven  by  increased  demand  for  Shariah-compliant
investments, sovereign funds’ diversification needs, development of regulatory frameworks for Sukuk and growth in Middle East

capital markets.  Nearly  80%  of  Fitch ‑rated Sukuk  carry  investment ‑grade ratings  and  have  little  or  no  history  of  default,
underscoring the sector’s strong fundamentals. These factors should help to instil confidence among investors, who can look at
Sukuk as central building blocks of their portfolio.

Convergence with sustainable investing trends
Sukuk and sustainable bonds share more common ground than their different origins suggest, making sustainable Sukuk a
natural bridge between Islamic finance and sustainable investing. This also helps explain Sukuk’s growing appeal beyond
traditional Muslim investors, as evidenced by Sustainable Sukuk issuance rising from USD 464mn in 2017 to USD 9bn in 2024,

representing a CAGR of 52.7%. The overlap starts with what Sukuk exclude: Shariah principles prohibit interest, excessive
uncertainty and speculation, and screen out sectors financing harmful industries such as tobacco, gambling, weapons, adult
entertainment and alcohol – a negative screen that closely aligns with the exclusionary (negative screening) method, commonly
used in sustainable investing. Tradi tional Sukuk bonds require proceeds to be linked to identifiable real assets or projects,

creating a ring-fenced structure like use-of-proceeds sustainable bonds. Combined with Shariah’s broader objectives of social
welfare, resource stewardship and equita ble risk-sharing, this creates strong alignment with the United Nations Sustainable
Development Goals.
Green Sukuk is an established example, where proceeds are earmarked for environmental projects such as renewable energy,
clean transport, energy-efficient buildings or sustainable water and wastewater management. Issuers must meet both Shariah

approval from a recognised scholarly board and alignment with International Capital Market Association (ICMA) Green Bond
Principles.
Traditional Sukuk already comply with Shariah laws such as avoiding Riba (interest -based returns arising from lending
transactions), Gharar (investments with ambiguity) and Maysir (speculation). Sustainable Sukuk build on this foundation by
adding environmental and social objectives. The table below highlights how sustainable Sukuk differ from traditional Sukuk.

Fig. 4
Traditional Sukuk vs. Sustainable bonds vs Sustainable Sukuk
Aspect Traditional Sukuk Sustainable Bonds Sustainable Sukuk
Underlying structure Asset-linked
Use of proceeds For any Shariah-compliant
purpose

Digital integration
The integration  of  blockchain  technology  and  digital  platforms  is  revolutionising  Sukuk  issuance  and  trading,  dramatically
reducing transaction costs and expanding accessibility to  a broader  investor base.  For instance, SAKKEX (an  independent
blockchain-based Sukuk marketplace) streamlines drafting, issuance, and near ‑instant settlement. Its Sukuk Wallet App also
allows investors to trade on the go. Several such digital platforms now allow tokenised Sukuk that enable fractional ownership,

automated Shariah  compliance  verification  and  real-time  settlement,  thereby  addressing  challenges  that  have  persisted  in
Islamic finance markets. Digital tools also simplify cross border Sukuk transactions by enabling end to end digital origination and
trading, with smart contracts automating profit sharing to cut operational costs and enhance transparency.

### Page 6

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026 6
Sukuk structures define the nature of ownership, risk -sharing and return
generation. Several types of Sukuk structures exist and are defined by the
underlying Shariah contract. We discuss some prominent ones below and see
how they align with various phases of an investor’s wealth journey:
Sukuk al-Ijarah is a Shariah-compliant lease-based bond where the investor gains partial ownership in a physical

asset such as a building, airport or equipment. The asset is then leased back to the issuer who pays a nominal
(pre-agreed) rent to the investor. An investor seeking stable, predictable income streams with lower volatility can
consider investing in these Sukuk. The original principal amount is returned at the end of the lease period.
Ijarah Sukuk are ideal for the wealth preservation phase of an investor’s wealth journey. For instance, investors
nearing retirement typically need to restructure their portfolios by shifting from growth to preservation while

generating steady income to cover living expenses. Ijarah Sukuk deliver on both fronts: protecting capital through
asset-backed structures while providing predictable rental income that supports household needs without
depleting principal.
Sukuk al-Murabahah are cost-plus financing structures where the issuer buys an asset and immediately resells
it to the investors at a marked-up price, with payment deferred over a pre-determined time. Consider this: Let us

assume, a company needs USD 100m. A special purpose vehicle (SPV), established by the company, mobilises
funds from investors and buys commodities worth USD 100m and immediately resells them to the company for
USD 112m payable over time. The USD 12m difference is the agreed profit margin. Murabahah Sukuk ar e best
suited for investors seeking income over a short to medium term, typically, 1-5 years. Investments in this type of
Sukuk return the principal gradually throughout the investment period rather than only at maturity.

Investments in Murabahah Sukuk are best suited for wealth protection and income generation. Unlike Ijarah which
return principal as a lump sum at maturity, Murabahah amortise both profit and principal in each instalment. This
makes them ideal for retirees who need to systematically draw down capital to fund living expenses without
depleting their portfolio too quickly.
Sukuk al-Mudarabah is a profit-sharing partnership arrangement where investors provide 100% of the capital

and operate as silent partners. The issuer manages the business or project as the active partner. Under a
Mudarabah arrangement, financial losses arising from normal bu siness risks are borne by the investors in
proportion to their capital contribution, while the Mudarib loses its effort and entitlement to profit. However, where
losses result from negligence, misconduct, breach of agreed terms, or misconduct by the Mudari b, the Mudarib
may be held liable. Think of it as venture capital meets bonds; you fund the business, professionals run it and

you share the profits or bear the losses resulting from the business activities. Mudarabah Sukuk are often
regarded as one of the  structures most closely aligned with the Shariah principle of profit and loss sharing.
However, they bear higher risk and lower predictability compared to other structures.
Investors with a growth mindset and open to high -risk tolerance can consider investing in these Sukuk . They
best align with accumulation and growth phase of the wealth journey, typically, when investors have long time

horizons to absorb volatility and can afford capital risk for higher returns. However, to balance risk, it is important
for investors to diversify across various types of Sukuk. Even within Mudarabah, they can consider investing
in multiple projects, sectors and geographies.
1 Sukuk structures and
wealth mandates

### Page 7

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026  7

A joint venture partnership structure where both investors and the issuer contribute capital is Sukuk al -
Musharakah. In this structure, both parties (issuer and investors) jointly provide capital, participate in managing 
the venture, and share profits or losses based on agreed upon ratios. This structure appeals to investors focused 
on growth, interested in active participation, and prepared to take on greater risk in pursuit of higher returns. 
Musharakah Sukuk are best suited for the growth and accumulation phase where investors are building wealth 
aggressively, comfortable with high risk and able to lock up capital for the long term (typically 5+ years).

Sukuk al-Wakalah is an agency arrangement where investors appoint a representative from the issuing company 
as their Wakil (agent) to invest their funds in specific Shariah-compliant assets or projects in exchange for a fee. 
This arrangement is similar to a mutual fund or ETF, where the agent manages a diversified pool of Shariah 
compliant assets on behalf of the investors. Investors benefit from professional fund management, diversification, 
transparency and flexibility. Depending on issuer preferences, Wakalah Sukuk can b e structured with lower 
investment thresholds, making them accessible to a wide range of investors. 
Wakalah Sukuk suitability depends heavily on the underlying asset allocation and risk profile. Conservative 
Wakalah structures investing in stable, income -generating assets suit wealth preservation. Moderate -risk 
Wakalah can complement accumulation portfolios as a diversification tool, though not as a primary growth driver 
as their structure and risk mandate limit upside potential.

Sukuk al-Istisna is a project financing structure based on a manufacturing or construction contract. Investors 
provide upfront capital to finance the construction of specific assets. Upon completion, the asset is sold to an end 
buyer (often the original issuer or a third party) who makes deferred payments including the cost plus a mark-up, 
which are then distributed to investors. Istisna suit seasoned investors with construction finance expertise and 
those who can absorb development risk and commit capital for the long term. Owing to its complexity and higher 
risk profile, this type of Sukuk typically requires large initial investments, making it less accessible to retail 
investors.  
In the wealth cycle, Istisna are best aligned with the accumulation and growth phase, when long time horizons 
can absorb construction delays and development risks. The long-term commitment and potential for cost overruns 
or project failure make it unsuitable for later wealth stages such as retirement.

Sukuk al -Salam is an Islamic bond backed by a forward purchase contract where investors pay upfront for 
commodities to be delivered later. At its core, they are similar to forward contracts. However, unlike a forward 
contract, Salam contracts are fully prepaid, standardi sed and designed with clearly defined delivery terms to 
reduce uncertainty (gharar). Under this arrangement, an investor profits from the difference between the lower 
Salam purchase price (paid today) and the higher resale price (received at delivery).  
Salam Sukuk carry high delivery and price risk and aligns well with investors in the growth and accumulation 
phase. Salam requires strong commodity‑market expertise, deep understanding of production and delivery risks, 
and the ability to assess the issuer’s operational capacity to deliver the specified goods on time.

Owing to their ethical framework and asset -backed nature, Sukuk are often
considered relatively less risky than conventional bonds. However, they are not
immune to risks. Unique challenges such as asset quality deterioration and
structure-specific exposures require rigorous risk management.

Due diligence in Sukuk extends beyond traditional credit
analysis and an investor should also consider the following:
• Shariah compliance verification can be done by evaluating

the Sukuk structure and confirming that its mandates and
underlying assets fully align with Islamic principles.
• Evaluating asset quality by verifying tangibility, cash -
generating capability and legal ownership can help
investor reduce risk.
• Assessing creditworthiness and market depth will help in
identifying financially strong Sukuk issuers and ensuring
adequate liquidity.
• Cross-border Sukuk investments remain viable,
contingent on careful scrutiny of legal documentation

across multiple jurisdictions to ensure enforceability and
investor protection.
Robust due diligence and proactive risk mitigation strategies
are essential to protect capital and stay aligned with Islamic
financial principles.
1 Risk management and due diligence

### Page 10

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026  10

Staying up to date on the latest developments in 
the Sukuk market is important as this space is 
constantly evolving and rapidly expanding. 
Moreover, Islamic finance is not static; Shariah 
interpretations related to Sukuk structures are 
periodically redefined. Staying informed protects 
investors from investing in non -compliant 
options. We look at some of the factors that are 
guiding the future of this landscape:

1 Emerging trends

Green Sukuk issuance is projected to grow materially through 
2030, reflecting the natural convergence of Islamic finance and 
sustainability principles. Key sectors include renewable 
energy, green buildings, low -carbon transportation, water and 
waste management and sustainable agriculture.

Tech advancements  are revolutionising Sukuk origination, 
trading and accessibility. Digitisation offers numerous benefits 
to the Islamic finance landscape: streamlined issuance and 
trading processes, enhanced accessibility through 
tokenisation, immutable audit trails via bl ockchain technology 
and improved secondary market liquidity.

A major regulatory shift is likely to create standardised rules 
across Islamic finance markets globally. The pivotal 
development is AAOIFI's Standard 62, launching in 2026, 
which mandates transparent, Shariah-compliant asset-backing 
and genuine asset ownership. This can fundament ally 
strengthen the Islamic authenticity of Sukuk structures.

The Sukuk market is rapidly expanding beyond its traditional 
Islamic finance strongholds. Non-traditional territories  in 
Africa (e.g., Algeria, Benin and Nigeria), Europe (e.g., 
Luxemburg, UK) and Asia (e.g., Turkey, Pakistan) are 
emerging as Islamic capital market gateways. This expansion 
underscores the evolution of Sukuk from a niche Islamic 
product to a globally reco gnised asset class attracting diverse 
sovereigns and investors across continents.

### Page 11

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026  11

Conclusion

Ultimately, Sukuk demonstrate that Shariah compliance and sound investment 
strategy are not competing priorities, but complementary ones. By ensuring that 
portfolios are grounded in real economic activity by offering stability through 
tangible asset -backing and transparent structures that channel capital towards 
productive use. Their foundation in ethical, responsible financing appeals to 
investors seeking both resilience and purpose. As global markets increasingly 
prioritise integrity and long-term sustainability, Sukuk stand out as a compelling tool 
for Shariah -compliant investors who want meaningful diversification without 
compromising on their religious and ethical principles.

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: US Economy Continues To Roar, While The Fed Waits For More
Date:    4 hours ago
US Economy Continues To Roar, While The Fed Waits For More

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
US Economy Continues To Roar,
While The Fed Waits For More
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 29 Oct 2026
View in browser
Photo by Pawan Sharma / Unsplash

### Page 2

The FOMC voted 10-2 on Wednesday to hold the federal funds rate at
3.75%-4.00%, with the two dissenters favoring a 25bps hike. Yet Fed
Chair Kevin Warsh's assessment of the US economy sounded decidedly
hawkish. Economic growth is robust and broadening, the labor market is
in good shape, inflation remains above the Fed's 2.0% y/y target, and
financial conditions are hardly tight. So why did the Fed stand pat?
Warsh argued that September's hike was already tightening financial

conditions, inflation expectations remained anchored, and stronger
productivity growth was allowing the economy to expand without
overheating. With an "information-rich" period ahead, the Fed saw little
reason to rush, he said.
In our view, the decision was puzzling. It reminds us of someone standing
in a room with a fire alarm blaring and smoke pouring under the door, yet
insisting on more evidence before calling the fire department. Thursday's
economic data only reinforced our conviction that the Fed should have

raised rates by 25bps on Wednesday. Let's take a closer look.
(1) GDP. The US economy expanded at a 3.4% annualized rate in Q3-
2026, the largest gain in a year (chart).
A closer look reveals remarkably strong underlying demand. Real final
sales to private domestic purchasers, excluding volatile trade and
inventory swings, surged 4.9% (saar), the best increase since Q2-2021
(chart)! Consumer spending advanced 3.8% for the second consecutive
quarter, its fastest pace since Q4-2024. Nonresidential fixed investment

### Page 3

soared 12.7%, a pace last exceeded outside the pandemic period in Q2-
1978. The AI investment boom was a major driver, with investment in
information-processing equipment surging 37.4% and software investment
rising 9.6%. Yet headline GDP understated this strength because net
exports subtracted 3.32 percentage points from growth. Imports surged
19.7%, led by AI-related durable goods. Exports fell 5.7%.
Consumer spending and business investment are driving the US

economic boom. Federal government spending is too, rising 15.8% last
quarter.
The Weekly Economic Index rose to 3.3% for the week ended October 16,
signaling a solid start to Q4's real GDP growth (chart).
(2) Inflation. In Q3's GDP report, core PCED inflation remained
troublesome at 3.0% y/y, well above the Fed's 2.0% target (chart). On the

### Page 4

brighter side, it rose at an annualized rate of 2.4% during the quarter.
Headline PCED inflation rose 0.5% m/m in September, lifting its yearly
rate to 3.6% (chart). Core PCED inflation, excluding food and energy,
increased 0.3% m/m and 3.1% y/y. Both remain stubbornly above the
Fed's inflation target. Furthermore, Warsh's inflation diffusion measure
shows that nearly half of PCED components rose more than 3.0% y/y,
compared with a pre-pandemic average of just 32%.

Goods PCED inflation rose to 3.9% y/y in September from 3.6% in August,
partly reflecting a 7.3% m/m increase in gasoline prices (chart).

### Page 5

Tariff-related price pressures have yet to subside, while the AI investment
boom is adding to inflation across the technology sector. Prices for
computers and software are rising at record annual rates (chart). Together,
these forces suggest goods inflation is unlikely to ease meaningfully soon.
Services PCED inflation rose to 3.5% y/y in September (chart). Supercore
services inflation, excluding housing and energy, edged up to 3.6% y/y
(chart). It has been trending higher since late 2025, underscoring

persistent underlying price pressures.

### Page 6

Services inflation remains broad-based, with most major categories rising
around 3.0% y/y or more. Transportation services inflation has been
particularly troublesome as higher energy costs feed through to service
prices (chart).
(3) Consumer spending and income. Americans continue to shop. Real
consumer spending remained at a record high in September, even as real
disposable personal income (DPI) declined for the first time since April
(chart). Real DPI has been essentially flat for over a year, a trend likely to

persist as more Baby Boomers retire.

### Page 7

September's saving rate declined to 3.8%, the lowest since 2022 (chart).
We expect it to fall further as retiring Baby Boomers continue spending
their sizeable net worth because they are no longer earning labor income.
Real consumer spending was flat in September, as a 0.3% m/m decline in
goods purchases offset a 0.2% increase in services spending. Spending
on restaurants and hotels remained particularly robust, rising 0.5%
following a 1.1% surge in August (chart).

### Page 8

(4) Labor market. Initial unemployment claims fell to just 194,000 during
the week of October 24, marking the tenth reading below 200,000 this
year, the most in any year since 1969! The four-week moving average
also fell to its lowest level since October 1969. Continuing claims also
declined, approaching their lowest level since April 2023. These figures
underscore the labor market's remarkable resilience (chart).

Document 436: USA_ Pushing Back Fed Rate Hike on Lower Core PCE Inflation; Q2 GDP Revised Up to +2.2%; Trade Deficit Widens More Than Expected; Lowering Q3 GDP Tracking to +3.3%

Source PDF: all report pdf/USA_ Pushing Back Fed Rate Hike on Lower Core PCE Inflation; Q2 GDP Revised Up to +2.2%; Trade Deficit Widens More Than Expected; Lowering Q3 GDP Tracking to +3.3%.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-09-30
Pages: 8
Status: ok

### Page 2

Personal consumption (third estimate) +3.8% (qoq ar) for Q2 vs. GS +3.4%, median
forecast +3.4%, prior revised +0.7%
Personal income +0.2% for August vs. GS +0.5%, median forecast +0.5%, prior revised
+0.3%
Personal spending +0.9% for August vs. GS +0.8%, median forecast +0.9%, prior revised
+0.1%
Advance goods trade balance -$132.6bn for August vs. GS -$116.0bn, median forecast
-$115.3bn, prior revised -$118.9bn
ADP employment +90k for September vs. GS +75k, median forecast +75k, prior revised

+36k
MAIN POINTS:
1. The core PCE index increased +0.25% in August, somewhat below expectations, and
the year-over-year rate increased 3.01%. We estimate that the Dallas Fed’s trimmed
mean PCE measure increased 0.16% month over month and 2.20% year over year.
2. Year-over-year core PCE was revised downward 0.36pp in July to 2.98%, primarily
reflecting the methodological revisions to the calculation of the portfolio management
(-0.23pp), computer accessories and software (-0.11pp), and legal services (+0.01pp)

components of PCE. These downward revisions were larger than we expected, mostly
reflecting a larger revision to the portfolio management component than we expected.
3. Today’s inflation report, especially the downward revisions resulting from the
methodological changes, imply that core PCE inflation is likely to substantially
undershoot the median FOMC participant’s forecast of 3.4% for 2026 on a Q4/Q4 basis.
We now expect a 3.0% increase for core PCE in 2026 on a Q4/Q4 basis. Coupled with

comments from New York Fed President John Williams yesterday that “with the policy
action we took at our September meeting, there is no need for urgency, and we have
time to gather more information,” we now think that an October hike is unlikely.  We are
pushing back the second hike in our forecast to December, and we see a strong chance
that the FOMC will ultimately conclude that additional rate hikes are unnecessary.
4. Real GDP was revised up by 0.7pp to +2.2% in Q2 (quarter-over-quarter annualized),

largely reflecting upward revisions to consumption (+0.4pp to +3.8%) and investment
(+1.9pp to +4.6%) growth. The strength in consumer spending in Q2 likely continued to
reflect the boost to consumer cashflow from larger-than-usual tax refunds, but we
continue to expect consumer spending growth to slow somewhat in 2026Q4 as the fiscal
support fades and the headwind from higher energy prices remains. Stronger
investment growth reflected an upward revision to private fixed investment (+0.5pp to

+9.0%), driven by data center investment in the nonresidential structures category
(+1.9pp to +0.1%), and an upward revision to the contribution from inventories (+0.2pp
to -0.5pp).
5. Reflecting today’s annual revisions to the national accounts, real GDP was revised up
by 0.4pp to +2.5% in Q1 (quarter-over-quarter annualized), largely reflecting upward
revisions to the contributions from consumption and exports. Revisions for prior years
were modest: real GDP growth on a Q4/Q4 basis was unrevised for 2025 (+2.0%) and

2023 (+3.4%), and slightly revised down for 2024 (-0.2pp to +2.4%) and up for 2022
30 September 2026   2
Goldman Sachs

### Page 3

(+0.1pp to +1.3%). 
6. Personal income increased 0.2% in August, below expectations. Year-over-year 
disposable personal income growth reached 4.8% in August. Personal spending rose 
0.9% in August, in line with consensus expectations. The savings rate decreased 0.5pp to 
4.1% in August, after a large 1.6pp upward revision to the savings rate last month driven 
by an upward revision to the level of disposable personal income (+1.2pp contribution) 
and a downward revision to personal spending as a share of DPI (+0.4pp). 
7. According to the Advance Economic Indicators Report, the goods trade deficit 
widened by $13.7bn to $132.6bn (seasonally adjusted) in August, well above 
expectations. Goods exports increased by $3.7bn to $203.4bn, largely driven by an 
increase in industrial supplies exports. Goods imports increased by $17.4bn to 
$336.1bn, driven by a large increase in industrial supplies and capital goods imports. 
8. According to the ADP report, private sector employment rose by 90k in September 
(month-over-month, seasonally adjusted), slightly above consensus expectations. 
Employment in the services sector rose 59k, primarily reflecting a 55k increase in 
education and health services and a 22k increase in leisure and hospitality employment 
that were partly offset by a 16k decrease in financial activities employment. 
Employment in goods-producing industries increased by 31k, driven by a 17k increase in 
manufacturing and a 15k increase in construction employment. ADP’s new median 
annual base pay measure, which better tracks other measures of wage growth than its 
gross pay measure, increased 3.0% for job stayers, unchanged from the August pace, 
and 4.8% for job changers, 0.1pp above the August pace. We left our forecast for August 
nonfarm payroll growth unchanged at +80k (vs. +90k consensus) ahead of Friday’s 
release. 
9. The details of the personal income and spending report were firmer than our previous 
GDP tracking assumptions. While the trade-related details of the Advance Economic 
Indicators report were much weaker than our previous assumptions, suggesting notably 
stronger imports growth in Q3 than we previously assumed, the inventories details were 
significantly stronger. On net, we lowered our Q3 GDP tracking estimate by 0.1pp to 
+3.3% (quarter-over-quarter annualized).

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
2
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
• The Westpac–DataX Card Tracker
Index* has edged down slightly since
late September, slipping 0.9pts to 160.6
in the week ended October 17. Despite
this, growth in activity continues to track
a robust pace with quarterly momentum

running at close to 2%qtr and annual
growth at 6%yr.
• Some of this reflects revisions. Initial
figures on card activity can see changes
in subsequent weeks due to information
lags. While these are usually small,
the cumulative impact can sometimes
be material. In this case, revisions have
lifted the index in late September by
2.5pts and the 4wk trend growth rate at
the time from 0.2% to 0.6%. We will be
back-testing regular revisions to assess
whether there is a systematic bias that

can be adjusted for in future.
• The revised profile shows quarterly
growth accelerating steadily since
June, posting a 1.9%qtr gain for the
September quarter and tracking a
2.1%qtr pace in the latest week. This is
back near the robust pace seen in late
2024 and early 2025.
• The monthly and weekly reads show
a softer 0.2%mth rise in August was
followed by a solid 0.8%mth gain in
September that has largely carried into
the first two weeks of October (the latest

observation tracking a 0.4%mth pace).
• The detail shows gains are not just
due to higher fuel prices with strong
growth in discretionary services and
international transactions over the last
quarter and steady growth in essential
services as well. To the extent that the
weekly and monthly data is showing
some renewed squeeze from higher fuel
prices, this is showing through most
clearly in discretionary goods, which
have seen activity decline over the last

six weeks, with discretionary services
still posting positive, albeit slower
growth. Quarterly growth in non-fuel
card activity is tracking around 1.4%qtr.
• Official September quarter figures on
inflation and household spending due
in the coming weeks should confirm the
spending momentum and shed more
light on the extent to which it is due to
prices or volumes.

running at close to 2%qtr ...”

1. Westpac–DataX Card Tracker Index*
Card activity showing robust growth but with a mixed start to October
The Westpac–DataX Card Tracker presents indicators based on the millions of
credit and debit card transactions processed by Westpac every day. The measures
are a timely guide to shifts in spending. See p 10 for a detailed explanation.
This report is produced by Westpac Economics.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
3
• Chart 2 shows how quarterly growth
in the Westpac–DataX Card Tracker
Index compares to growth in nominal
consumption as reported in the national
accounts. Growth has shown a clear
acceleration since late June, lifting to
1.9%qtr in the September quarter and

running at 2.1%qtr in the latest week.
Weekly reads in early October have been
the strongest since July 2025.
• Only some of this reflects a price-
driven rise in fuel spend. The unwind of
temporary fuel excise tax cuts in August
and more recent surge in global energy
prices has seen local pump prices push
back above $2.40/litre locally, up from
$1.56/litre in late June.
• Excluding fuel, quarterly growth in card
activity has moderated, but only slightly,

from 1.9%qtr in August to 1.8% in
September with the latest week tracking
a 1.4% quarterly pace. The impact is
more pronounced in monthly terms with
a –0.4%mth decline in August, muted
+0.2% gain in September and subdued
+0.1% monthly pace in the latest week
(c.f. total: –0.4%; +0.2% and +0.1%).
• The 1.9%qtr rise in nominal terms and an
expected price rise of 1.1%qtr point to a
real, inflation-adjusted gain of 0.8%qtr,
in line with Westpac’s forecasts. ABS Q3

CPI figures are due shortly after we go to
press with the Q3 household spending
indicator due Nov 4 and GDP due Dec 2.

• Chart 4 shows quarterly growth in card
activity broken down by contributions
from major state and category groups.
• The contribution from essential goods
captures the effect of fuel price swings.

have been the main driver of growth

services. Discretionary goods activity
has essentially stalled flat. In quarterly
growth terms, essential goods is now
leading, running at 3.6%qtr (+0.9%qtr ex
fuel), with essential services tracking at
2.5%qtr, discretionary services running
at 2.0%qtr and discretionary goods much
weaker at –0.1%qtr (down materially in
per capita terms).
• By state, the large south eastern states
of NSW and Vic account for nearly two

thirds of the growth.
• As noted, the latest update includes
significant revisions. Data lags and
cleaning often result in shifts to historical
estimates. These are typically small but
can be have a material cumulative effect.
Chart 5 shows the impact of the latest
revision: for the last week of September, it
lifts the WCTI 2.5pts and the 4wk trend
growth rate from 0.2% to 0.6%, removing
what looked to be a slowing in momentum
at the time. We will be investigating this

issue more closely in coming weeks to see
whether there is a systematic bias that
can be adjusted for in the future.

• Our MHSI and retail card indexes 
are composite measures based on 
transactions in categories that are in 
scope for the ABS monthly household 
spending indicator (MHSI) and retail 
sales estimates (which have been based 
on the MHSI detail since the ABS retail 
survey was discontinued in June 2025). 
• After under-performing the broader 
WCTI in Q2, the MHSI index has moved 
more in line in Q3, posting growth of 
1.6%qtr with the latest week running  at 
2%qtr. The retail card index has been 
more subdued, up 1.1%qtr in Q3, growth 
running at just 0.4%qtr in the latest week.
• The ABS MHSI has been firmer than 
our index since June, mainly reflecting 
strong gains in vehicle sales which are 
not incorporated into our index. Vehicle 
sales appear to have been relatively flat in 
recent months, our estimates suggesting 
the official measure, due Nov 4, should 
show quarterly growth more in line with 
our indicator (Chart 7).
• The more granular detail on the retail 
card index shows non-food retail 
contracting materially over the last four 
weeks, consistent with the weakness 
being observed across discretionary 
goods categories.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS 6
• The Westpac Card Tracker draws on
a rich dataset that sheds light on many
aspects of consumer demand. In some
cases, it can also reveal some nuances
about the way spend is captured – in
official statistics, industry measures and
the card data itself. Vehicles provide a

particularly good example of this.
• Vehicle spend has long been a
bellwether for the wider consumer cycle.
As a large discretionary expenditure, it is
more sensitive to interest rate changes,
‘wealth effects’ and sentiment shifts,
particularly around job security. Given
this, ABS measures and industry figures
on vehicle sales have delivered material
upside surprises over the last year.
Vehicle purchases recorded a remarkable
10.3%qtr surge in the June quarter to be

up 7.3%yr with industry figures showing
vehicle sales holding around these levels
in the September quarter. Even allowing
‘extenuating circumstances’ driving
strong demand for EVs, the results seem
to fly in the face of wider interest rate,
cost-of-living and sentiment narratives.
• The card tracker data allows for a deeper
dive. Note that credit and debit cards are
not often used to purchase vehicles with
account transfers or financing typically

used for the main purchase instead.
• However, cards are used for up-front
payments. This shows through in
payment sizes, which are in the hundreds
of dollars (average $750) not the tens of
thousands. As such, card activity should
still give some guide, particularly around
sales volumes.
• Chart 8 shows how transactions have
tracked and highlight another important
point. Most of the data in this space
focuses on new vehicles (for the ABS
this is because used sales are a transfer

between households that ‘cancels out’ for
the sector as a whole). However, industry
estimates show used sales are both more
common and have been tracking a weaker
profile – the card data suggests used sales
are down at least 3%yr (finer estimates
putting the figure closer to 8%yr).
• Age-groups offer another insightful lens.
Activity has been much firmer across
older age groups, particularly in the ‘new
vehicle’ segment where they are more
prominent (accounting for 42% of all

activity) and activity is up on a year ago.
This group has notably stronger balance
sheets and is low exposure to interest
rate rises. In contrast, vehicle spend has
been contracting materially across other
age-groups, particularly used car spend
by the ‘mortgage belt’ 35-54 year olds.
Vehicle-related card activity highlights diverging trends

WESTPAC ECONOMICS 10
About the Westpac card data indicators
The indicators presented in this report are based on the millions of credit and debit card
transactions processed every day. Transactions covering over ten million merchants are
classified into over 700 categories. These are in turn grouped into higher level aggregates

that provide a timely guide to wider economic trends.
The main focus of these indicators is consumer spending. Where possible, we have sought
to exclude ‘non spending’ transactions such as: money transfers; tax payments; loan
repayments; charitable donations; and superannuation contributions.
It should also be noted that these indicators will also be affected by shifts between card and
non-card transactions. This was a significant factor during the COVID–19 pandemic – health

concerns about the use of physical cash leading to significantly higher use of cards vs cash,
particularly where contact–less transactions are available. Transaction flows also include
reversals/refunds which were also a significant phenomenon during the onset of COVID,
especially in areas such as travel.
All transaction data is compiled at a highly aggregated level so that individual customer or
merchant data is never revealed.
Index construction

The key metrics used in this report are indexes of spending–related card activity where the
base of 100 is average activity in 2019. As an example, if transaction flows are 5% above their
average level in 2019, the index read for the period is 105. If flows in a subsequent period are
8% above the average level in 2019, the index read for this period is 108. Growth between the
two periods can be calculated simply as the change between the two index reads, i.e. 2.9%.

All measures are adjusted for regular seasonality. Weekly estimates are generated using
the US Bureau of Labor’s MoveReg weekly seasonal adjustment program. Note that in some
cases, high levels of volatility during the COVID period mean it is not possible to produce
seasonally adjusted estimates for some historical periods.
Also, note that previous versions of this report used different approaches to seasonal
adjustment and measurement more generally. This means Index reads are sometimes not

directly comparable. See the ‘About the Westpac card data indicators’ sections from earlier
reports to more detail.
Classifications
Note that the measures used for card data and in this report do not align completely with
the those used in official ABS statistics, including the ABS household spending indicator,
ABS retail trade survey and ABS estimate of consumer spending published in the national
accounts. There are a range of differences including around both coverage and classification.

As such, the card data should be treated as broadly indicative.
The transaction data is grouped into 26 categories that are then combined into four main as
follows:
Discretionary goods: alcoholic beverages, tobacco, clothing & footwear, furnishings &
household equipment, household appliances, vehicle–related, recreational & cultural goods,
newspaper, books & stationery, and other personal effects.
Discretionary services: transport services (part), recreational & cultural services, gambling,

catering services, accommodation services, other personal care, insurance & financial
services, other services.
Essential goods: food & non alcoholic beverages, medical products, appliances & equipment,
and operation of personal transport equipment.
Essential services: electricity, gas & other fuels, health services, transport services (part),
communications and education.
The report also uses two additional classifications:
MHSI/Retail: based on the extent to which categories cover activity that is in scope for the

ABS monthly household spending indicator and ABS retail trade survey.

《盤勢看法》
科技類股於10月中旬出現明顯修正 ，不過市場資金
並未明顯撤離 ，而是在其他類股輪動 ，使標普500
指數的修正幅度相對有限 。長期來說，穩健的經濟
基本面才是股市行情延續的關鍵 ，而目前的景氣和
企業獲利支持這樣的趨勢 ，只是高利率環境仍將使
市場資金更加嚴格看待資產的成長性 。拉美方面 ，
現任左翼總統魯拉在 10月初第一輪投票落後的情況
下，於第二輪決選中逆轉 ，市場重新定價財政前景 ，
利率上升壓力將提高 。據此，將拉美和巴西股市的
評等由正向下調為中立。

《貨幣政策-聯準會偏鷹發言但不急於連續升息》
10月FOMC會議如市場預期維持政策利率在3.75%-
4.0%不變，本次會議的重點可歸納如下：
◼ 聯準會認為美國經濟非常強勁 ，消費支出健康 ，
企業投資強勁 ，通膨仍處於偏高水準 ，「但也
不急於立刻升息 」。華許的解釋並不是通膨風
險已經消失 ，而是認為先前的政策行動已經透
過金融條件產生緊縮效果 ，投資人開始更加審
慎地配置資金，部分資產的信用利差擴大。
◼ 縮減資產負債表不是當前政策的優先選項 ，相
關問題仍交由工作小組研究 ，這表示雖然他在
長期制度設計上支持重新檢討聯準會資產負債
表的規模與功能 ，也不代表短期內就會立即推
動更激進的量化緊縮。
雖然華許一如既往地不提供對未來利率路徑的承諾 ，

但從記者會的發言可大致推論 ，目前的方案是間隔
一次會議再採取下一個新的行動 ，本次會議暫停升
息，12月會議將進行下一次升息 。2027年1月的會
議按兵不動，3月會議視市場情況再決定。本行的基
準情境是，2027年開始會看到關稅效應 、能源衝擊
對通膨的影響逐漸消退 ，因此不認為 2027年3月有
升息的必要性 。風險則在於美伊衝突進一步升級 ，
使企業利用年初例行漲價的機會將成本壓力轉嫁給
消費者，若此，3月可能再升息一次。但本行仍定調
9月以來的升息決策為「風險管理式的升息」，而非
持續性的升息循環 。加上華許並未將縮減資產負債
表列入優先事項，過度緊縮的風險不大。
重點摘要
• 本行仍定調9月以來的升息決
策為 「風險管理式的升息 」，
而非持續性的升息循環 。加
上華許並未將縮減資產負債
表列入優先事項 ，過度緊縮
的風險不大
2

《總經情勢和殖利率-景氣穩健但不到過熱程度，美
債壓力有緩和跡象》
美國3Q26 GDP季增年率3.4%，高於2Q26的2.2%，
主要受惠於消費表現優於預期 。在油價高漲 、利率
上揚的環境當中 ，美國消費的強勁程度相當出乎意
料。其實個人所得成長率 、就業動能，都僅是溫和
成長，整體消費的動能 ，和股市帶來的財富效應息
息相關。數據顯示 ，美國家庭持有的股權價值已接
近年稅後所得的 3倍。但這也帶來一些疑慮 ，如k型
消費、景氣對股市的敏感度太高等 。綜合來看 ，美
國的經濟表現不錯，但並不到過熱程度。
美國10年期公債殖利率自 7月開始一波上行趨勢 ，
10月上旬一度升破 5.3%，但近期已回落到 5%之下，
一部分原因是市場對貨幣政策的共識已經開始凝聚 ，

以一季進行一次利率調整為原則 ，目前的經濟數據
尚不足以改變這點；另外原因是如同華許所述 ，長
率上升、金融條件往收緊的方向 ，已經透過融資成
本、信用利差 、投資人風險偏好來影響資本配置 。
當然這並不意味著公債利率將開啟下行趨勢 ，地緣
風險和財政問題仍不時帶來干擾 ，不過至少就近期
走勢而言 ，美債市場面臨的壓力已有初步緩和跡象 。
重點摘要
• 美國的經濟表現不錯 ，但並
不到過熱程度 。市場對貨幣
政策的共識已經開始凝聚 ，
以一季進行一次利率調整為
原則 ，美債市場面臨的壓力
有初步緩和跡象
2
資料來源: BCA，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10
消費動能越來越受財富效應影響
各輪景氣循環中，資產價值增幅占可支配所得的比例

《美股盤勢-景氣穩健但不過熱，美股短線有壓然無
礙趨勢》
科技類股於10月中旬出現明顯修正 ，尤其半導體相
關類股面臨較大的估值壓力 ，不過市場資金並未明
顯撤離 ，而是在其他類股輪動 ，使標普500指數的
修正幅度相對有限。
本行先前提及 「美股通常會在升息初期承壓 ，但若
非如同2022年般的連續性升息，股市的趨勢不至於
受到影響 」，此定調仍延續 。雖然市場不時會處於
「壞消息才是好消息 」的情緒當中 ，看到表現不錯
的經濟數據反而造成市場的震盪修正 ，但長期來說 ，
穩健的經濟基本面才是股市行情延續的關鍵 ，而目
前的景氣和企業獲利支持這樣的趨勢 ，只是高利率
環境仍將使市場資金更加嚴格看待資產的成長性 ，
具有強健資產負債表使其較不易受到融資成本上升

影響、具防禦性或差異化成長動能的企業 ，將能維
持相對韌性。
重點摘要
• 雖然市場不時會處於 「壞消

《拉美股市-巴西魯拉意外逆轉勝選，市場重新定價
財政前景，股市評等下調為中立》
巴西總統大選出現戲劇性逆轉 ，現任左翼總統魯拉
在10月初第一輪投票落後的情況下 ，於第二輪決選
中逆轉，擊敗右翼對手小波索納洛 ，這是1989年恢
復總統直選以來首次出現第二輪決選逆轉 ，巴西股
債匯市均出現劇烈下跌。
並不能說魯拉當選對巴西的經濟不利 ，他的前次當
選也並未引起股市震盪 。巴西股市在估值的吸引力 、
礦產的發展 ，都仍具有長線優勢 。然目前資本市場
確實較青睞較為親美和親商的波索納洛，巴西股市8
月中以來的漲幅不小 ，很大程度是反映波索納洛取
得優勢，因此市場需要修正先前的定價 ，外資撤出。
另外，魯拉第一輪投票後為挽救選情 ，承諾大量財

政政策，如擴大所得稅扣除額 、提高家庭補助金等 ，
在全球主要國家多面臨財政風險之際 ，市場對財政
前景的檢視力道將加大 ，利率上升壓力將提高 。據
此，將拉美股市和巴西股市的評等由正向下調為中
立。
重點摘要
• 巴西魯拉意外逆轉勝選 ，市
場重新定價財政前景 ，利率
上升壓力將提高 。據此 ，將
拉美股市和巴西股市的評等
由正向下調為中立。

（注）  作成時点で、 第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所経済調査部が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、 その正確性、 完全性に対す
る責任は負いません。
米国中間選挙は、 大統領任期4年の中間である2年目に行われる連邦議会選挙です。 2026年11月3日に実施され、 下

院の全435議席と上院の約3分の1が改選対象となります。 政権に対する 「中間評価」 と しての意味合いを持ち、 今後の
政策運営に大きな影響を与えます。
本選に先立って行われた各党の候補者選び （予備選挙） では、 トランプ大統領の後押しを受けた共和党候補が相次い
で勝ち上がった一方、 民主党でも現職政権への対決姿勢を鮮明にする候補が選ばれるなど、 両党ともに熱心な支持層
の意向が強く出た結果となりました。 しかし本選に向けては、 高止まりする物価や生活費負担への不満を背景に トランプ
大統領の支持率が低下傾向にあると報じ られるなど、 政権側には逆風も吹いています。 有権者の間では、 長引く中東情
勢の悪化を受けたインフレや雇用環境といった 「生活防衛」 が最大の投票判断基準となっているほか、 国境管理 ・ 不法移
民問題や人工妊娠中絶の権利といった社会的テーマも、 勝敗の鍵を握る激戦区 （スイング ・ ステー ト） での浮動票の行方
を左右する重要な争点です。
執筆時点の情勢と しては、 上院は共和党優位、 下院は接戦ながら民主党がやや有利とみられています。 先行きへの影

響と しては、 共和党が上下両院の過半数を維持する 「 ト リプルレ ッ ド」 が続けば、 トランプ政権の政策実行力が高まり規制
緩和などが進みやすくなる一方、 関税引き上げや財政悪化への懸念が残ります。 反対に、 民主党が下院を制して 「ねじれ
議会」 となった場合は、 法案や予算が通りにく くなり政権運営が停滞する反面、 過度な強硬策にブレーキがかかる点がか
えって安心材料と受け止められる側面もあります。 中間選挙の結果は、 米国の国内政治にとどまらず、 今後の通商政策
や金利の先行きを見極める分岐点となります。 （主席エコノミス ト ：  阿原健一郎）
11月の政治 ・ 経済イベン ト 「米国中間選挙」
4第一ライフ研レポー ト 2026.11

鋼鐵                     
中鋼(2002 TT)            法說 /座談摘要  
4Q26盤價上漲及鋼市展望轉好，評價仍偏低  
⚫ 中鋼自結 09/2026本業好轉，累計獲利轉虧為盈  
⚫ 4Q26盤價上漲，旺季接單及稼動率提升   
⚫ 國際鋼市仍佳且 全球鋼市復甦， 目前 PBR評價相對偏低  
結論：中鋼 3Q26本業營業利益轉好，4Q26盤價上漲營運動能展望轉好，
原物料成本推升， 12/2026 月盤價預估可望朝平高走勢，投顧預估中鋼
2026/2027 年 EPS各約 0.22/0.63 元，預估至 2027年底每股淨值 (BVPS)
約 20.23元，過去 10年中鋼平均 PBR約 1.27倍，以 2027年 BVPS計算
PBR約 0.97倍，PBR評價仍相對偏低，目標價 22元(2026年 PBR 1.1
倍)。 
中信證券座談  
1. 中鋼自結 09/2026合併營收 310.01億元，較上月 10.9%，主因原本
8月出貨遞延至 9月出貨，使得9月銷售量明顯成長，中鋼個體 62.4
萬噸，中鋼與中龍合計出貨 104.1萬噸。  
2. 09/2026 營業利益方面因 ASP下滑，鋼品毛利率下滑， 9月營業利
益減少到 4.7億元，業外 9月領到加拿大股利收入，稅前獲利 5.83
億元。  
3. 稼動率方面 4Q26接單狀況與 3Q26相比溫和提升，接單方面 3Q約
270萬噸，預期 4Q26接單量 280萬噸，稼動率方面 3Q26約 80%，
4Q可以再往上一些。  
4. 礦石成本方面 3Q26平均 96.9USD，較 2Q平均 105USD；煤炭成本
3Q平均約 236.3USD，2Q26平均 232USD，估計 4Q前期原料成本
下滑一點。  
5. 煤價因為中國 05/2026 及 08/2026 有礦災影響，山西煤礦場安檢嚴
格停產，推升煤礦價格提升，目前漸漸復產，4Q以來平均 270USD，
仍有 30~40USD價差，可能 4Q後半反映上來。  
6. 精緻鋼品方面 2026年原本預期 94.4萬噸，截至 1H26約 39萬噸，
目前預期努力能接近預期水準；展望 2027 年精緻鋼品先前預估
115.6 萬噸，後續會根據市場狀況微調，目前尚未正式對外公布對
外目標，預估可比 2026年再增加。  
7. 中國今年因為整體房產需求不好，製造業雖有改善，仍無法抵銷房
產下滑，中國鋼價普遍偏低， 8月中熱軋 3,260RMB，進到 9月微
幅上升至 3,340RM B，後續因需求不好，到近期 10 月又回到
3,206RMB。

8. 台灣當時因為進入旺季補庫有積極一些，流通行情有提升，熱軋 8 月

法說會重點摘要

環宇-KY 
(4991 TT, NT$ 367.5,  未評等)

重點摘要  
1. 3Q26 營收 US$26.4mn （新台幣 8.45 億元），QoQ +8.4% 、YoY 
+46.5% ；InP 基板與磊晶供應回穩， AOC 營收 QoQ +25.8% ，營
收比重由 2Q26 的 61%回升至 71%，帶動毛利率由 43.3% 回升至
47.0% ，營業利益率 19.3% ， EPS 1.33 元。產能利用 率超過
100% 
2. 4Q26 財測營收 US$30 -34mn 、毛利率 46-50% 、營業費用
US$7.6- 8.6mn ，動能來自 AOC 需求持續強勁及威力富新增產能。
200G PD 驗證已全數完成，已有客戶開始上量，導入第二家基板來
源後預期 2Q27 起有顯著營收貢獻；100mW CW Laser 依客戶回饋
調整，預計 3Q27 小批量、4Q27 大批量出貨 
3. 公司 2027 年 InP 基板已全數鎖定，變數在出口核准流程，非中國
基板來源預計 2028 年後才達有意義比例；威力富增資擴產與美國
廠無塵室擴充預計 2Q27 完成。 
結論 
我們認為 3Q26 基板供應回穩已使 AOC 出貨與毛利率重回正軌，
4Q26 財測中位數 US$32mn 隱含 QoQ 約 21% 成長，在稼動率超過
100% 、2027 年基板已鎖定下，短期營收動能明確。 2027 年的關鍵驗
證點在於 200G PD 於 2Q27 放量、100mW CW Laser 於 2H27 出貨，
以及擴產於 2Q27 如期完成；非中國基板供應仍需時間、出口核准可
能遞延，費用亦將隨 CW Laser 攤銷與 RSA 酬勞成本墊高。整體而
言，我們看好公司在 AI 資料中心光通訊需求擴張下，憑藉 InP PD 規格
優勢與產能布局延續穩定成長

經營環境與供應鏈策略  
 公司以美國為主要生產基地，認為未來若有政治面限制，對公司屬
正面 
 2Q26 的 InP 基板供應問題目前已趨穩定，但公司不認為未來必然
維持穩定，持續開拓不同基板來源、vendor 與生產基地，確保供應
再度受限時仍能應變並持續供貨 
 產能稼動率超過 100% ，供不應求； 11/3 董事會通過對威力富增
資，以增加產能並補齊設備，定位為美國以外的重要生產基地；美
國廠亦將擴充 cleanroom 並補齊瓶頸機台，預計 2Q27 完成

3Q26 財務表現  
 3Q26 營收新台幣 8.45 億元，QoQ 以美元計+8.37% 、以新台幣計
+9.7% ；YoY 以美元計+46.5% 、以新台幣計+55.4%

 毛利率 47.0% ，QoQ +3.7ppts ，主因： 
 AOC 銷量增加，營收比重由 2Q26 的 61%升至 71%

 認列的存貨跌價及呆滯損失減少 
 營業費用 2.35 億元，QoQ -0.2% 、YoY +53% ，主因 RSA 依會計
準則認列較高酬勞成本，以及員工與董事酬勞估列增加；營業費用
率 27.8% ，研發費用率由 2Q26 的 10.1% 降至 6.9% 
 營業利益 1.63 億元，營業利益率 19.3% ；稅後淨利 1.55 億元，歸
屬母公司淨利 1.59 億元，EPS 1.33 元（2Q26 0.92 元、3Q25 0.13
元） 
 業外收入 3,120 萬元，QoQ -12.2% ，主因對上海宙鎵光電認列的
投資利益減少；YoY 由虧轉盈，因自 1Q26 起公司對另一家轉投資
公司不再具重大影響力、改列 FVOCI ，認列的投資損失減少  
 1-9M26 營收 23.29 億元，YoY 以美元計+52.1% ；毛利率
48.1% ，YoY +3.7ppts ；稅後淨利 4.56 億元，EPS 3.91 元（2025
年同期- 0.45 元 ）；ROE 15.63%  
 9/30/26 現金及約當現金 25.10 億元，YoY +144% ，來自營業現金
流入與 1Q26 現金增資；3Q26 營業現金流 1.03 億元，低於 2Q26
的 2.61 億元，主因應收帳款增加尚未收現，應收帳款周轉天數 45
天，仍在合理授信期間內

 AOC ：QoQ +25.8% ，2Q26 受基板供應限制導致磊晶原料短缺，
3Q26 基板與磊晶供應回穩後銷量回升；YoY +39% ，受惠 data 
center 與 AI 需求增加 
 RF Foundry ：QoQ -7.64% ，因 1Q26 部分出貨遞延至 2Q26 墊高
基期；YoY +98% ，來自平均代工價格提高與出貨增加 
 Opto Foundry ：QoQ -37%，公司調整接單客戶，改以重要客戶或
未來預計投片生產的客戶為主；YoY -2.2% 
 3Q26 營收比重 RF Foundry 20% 、 Opto Foundry 6% 、 AOC 
71%、其他 3%
